(\phi^{-1})_{*}F
-flops,
as
\phi
contracts a curve that is disjoint from all the exceptional loci.
From Corollary
5.38
it follows that
n=0
. Hence a sequence as (
7.2
) does not exist.
∎

Lemma 7.3
.

Let
\mathcal{Y}
be a model of the DNV family of degree
2
with
\mathcal{Y}_{c}\in\operatorname{PMod}_{2}(\mathscr{T})
. Assume
\mathcal{Y}
is not symmetric. Then there sequence of type I flops

\mathcal{Y}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{Y}_{1}\dashrightarrow\dots\dashrightarrow\mathcal{Y}_{n}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{Y}

such that the composition
\gamma
of these flops is not an automorphism.

Proof.

Write
\mathcal{Y}_{c}=Y_{1}\cup Y_{2}\cup Y_{3}
with
Y_{1}
the special component. As
\mathcal{Y}
is not symmetric,
Y_{2}
and
Y_{3}
are not isomorphic.
Given
\mathcal{Y}
, by Proposition
5.32
, there exists a sequence
\phi\colon\mathcal{Y}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{Y}_{\mathscr{T}}
of type I flops. Necessarily,
\phi
maps the special component to the special component.
Let
\psi
be the automorphism from Proposition
5.45
. Consider the composition
\gamma=\phi^{-1}\circ\psi\circ\phi\colon\mathcal{Y}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{Y}
. By construction,
\gamma
maps
Y_{2}
to
Y_{3}
and
Y_{3}
to
Y_{2}
. As these components are not isomorphic, it follows that
\gamma

is not an automorphism. Being a composition of small modifications,
\gamma
has a factorisation

(7.3)

\mathcal{Y}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{Y}_{1}\dashrightarrow\dots\dashrightarrow\mathcal{Y}_{n}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{Y}

into
F
-flops for a divisor
F\in\operatorname{Mov}(\mathcal{Y}/S)
. Note that the smooth component
D_{\omega}
of the restriction of the double curve of
\mathcal{Y}
to
Y_{1}
is disjoint from
\operatorname{Ex}(\gamma)
. Hence, by Corollary
5.12
, all flops in the factorisation (
7.3
) are of type I and we thus obtain the desired sequence.
∎

Proposition 7.4
.

The topological space
\mathcal{M}
has
4
connected components. These are given by
\mathcal{C}_{\mathscr{P}}
and
3
components of type
\mathscr{T}
.

Proof.

Here we chose
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathscr{T}}
as a reference model and hence
consider
\operatorname{MF}(\mathcal{Y}_{\mathscr{T}}/S)
. By Proposition
5.43
, there are 3 cones in the orbit
of
\operatorname{Nef\textbullet}(\mathcal{Y}_{\mathscr{T}}/S)
. We can write these as
C(f_{1})
,
C(f_{2})
,
C(f_{3})
where

f_{1}=\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathscr{T}}}
and
f_{2},f_{3}\in\operatorname{Bir}(\mathcal{Y}_{\mathscr{T}}/S)
are birational
automorphisms of
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathscr{T}}\to S
. Let
\mathcal{C}_{i}

denote the connected component of
\mathcal{M}
such
that
C(f_{i})\subset\bar{\mathcal{C}_{i}}
. By Lemma
7.2
applied to
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathscr{T}}
, the
\mathcal{C}_{i}
are all distinct.
Let
\sigma
be a cone associated to a model
\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}
of class

\mathscr{T}
. We will show that
there is an
i\in\{1,2,3\}
such that
\sigma\subset\bar{\mathcal{C}_{i}}
. This implies the
result, as all cones such that the associated
model has class
\mathscr{P}

are
contained in the closure of
\mathcal{C}_{\mathscr{P}}
.
By Corollary
5.32
,
there is a composition of type I flops
f\colon\mathcal{Y}_{\mathscr{T}}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}
. This defines cones

C(f\circ f_{i})
,
i=1,\dots 3
with associated model
\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}
. The cone
C(f\circ f_{i})
is contained in
\bar{\mathcal{C}_{i}}
by construction. So we are done if
\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}
is symmetric, as there are then precisely
3
cones with associated model
\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}
, by Proposition
5.43
.

Suppose
\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}
is not symmetric. By Proposition
5.43
, there are
6
cones associated to

\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}
. We shall show that each of these cones lies in some
\bar{\mathcal{C}_{i}}
.
Since
\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}
is not symmetric, it follows from Lemma
7.3
that there is a non-trivial sequence of type I flops

\gamma\colon\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}
. Then
C(\gamma\circ f\circ f_{i})

is not equal to

C(f\circ f_{i})
,
as
\gamma
is not an automorphism and

C(\gamma\circ f\circ f_{i})\subset\bar{\mathcal{C}_{i}}
by
definition of
\mathcal{C}_{i}
. This shows that all cones associated to
\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}
are contained in some
\bar{\mathcal{C}_{i}}
.

∎

7.2.
Flopping along a line

Recall that we are, by Theorem
2.4
, in a Mori Dream space situation (in degree
2
).
The following is then a standard construction:
let
F
be a
\mathbb{Q}
-divisor in
\operatorname{Mov}(\mathcal{Y}/S)
. Suppose
F
is not nef on
\mathcal{Y}

and there is a cone
\sigma_{F}\in\operatorname{MF}_{\operatorname{max}}(\mathcal{Y}/S)
with
F\in\operatorname{Int}\sigma_{F}
.
Let
A
be an ample divisor and define
L=L(F,A)
to be the line segment connecting
F
and
A
.
Since
L
is spanned by interior points of a convex cone it is itself contained in the interior.
Suppose that for any facet
\tau\in\operatorname{MF}(\mathcal{Y}/S)
,
L\cap\tau\neq\varnothing
implies
L\cap\tau\subset\operatorname{Relint}(\tau)
. This means that the line
L
intersects maximal cones and their facets as nicely as possible.
Note that (by convexity), this implies that if
\sigma\in\operatorname{MF}_{\operatorname{max}}(\mathcal{Y}/S)
, at most two facets of a cone
\sigma
are met, and the only maximal cones
\sigma
such that there is a unique facet
\tau\subset\sigma
meeting
L
are
\sigma_{F}
and
\sigma_{A}=\operatorname{Nef\textbullet}(\mathcal{Y}/S)
. Let
\{\gamma_{i}\}_{i}
be the collection of maximal cones of
\operatorname{MF}(\mathcal{Y}/S)
such that
L\cap\operatorname{Int}\gamma_{i}\neq 0
. Note that this collection is finite by Theorem
2.4
(i).
Denote the unique facet of
\sigma_{A}
met by
L

by
\tau
and let
R
be the extremal ray corresponding to
\tau
. Then
F
is stricly negative on
R
. Consider the contraction morphism
\operatorname{contr}_{R}\colon\mathcal{Y}\to\mathcal{Z}
. It is a small contraction as
\tau
is interior and hence defines an
F
-flop
\phi\colon\mathcal{Y}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{Y}^{+}
.
This gives a canonical linear isomorphism
\phi_{*}\colon\operatorname{N}^{1}(\mathcal{Y}/S)\to\operatorname{N}^{1}(\mathcal{Y}^{+}/S)
.
Choose an ample divisor
A^{+}\in\operatorname{Pic}(\mathcal{Y}^{+}/S)
on
\phi_{*}(L)
. Now we can consider the divisor
\phi_{*}F
and the line segment
L(\phi_{*}F,A^{+})
and repeat the argument for this data. In this way we obtain a finite sequence of
F
-flops

\mathcal{Y}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{Y}_{1}\dashrightarrow\dots\dashrightarrow\mathcal{Y}_{n}.

We will call this sequence a
sequence of F-flops induced by
L
.
We note that this may depend on the choice of
A
and hence
L
, but not on the choice of
A^{+}
and subsequent choices.
The truncations
\phi_{l}\colon\mathcal{Y}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{Y}_{l}
define maximal cones of
\operatorname{MF}(\mathcal{Y}/S)
and by construction, for each
\gamma_{i}
there is an
l
such that
\gamma_{i}=\phi_{l}^{*}(\operatorname{Nef\textbullet}(\mathcal{Y}_{l}/S))
. Note also that
\gamma_{n}=\sigma_{F}
by construction.

Definition 7.5
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a connected component of
\mathcal{M}
with closure

\bar{\mathcal{C}}
.
A
\mathcal{C}
-test segment
is a line segment
L(p,q)
such that

p,q
are points in
\mathcal{C}
, such that there are cones
\sigma_{p},\sigma_{q}\in\operatorname{MF}_{\operatorname{max}}(\mathcal{Y}/S)
with
p\in\operatorname{Int}(\sigma_{p})
and
q\in\operatorname{Int}(\sigma_{q})
.
A test segment
L
is called
nice
if for any interior facet
\tau\in\operatorname{MF}(\mathcal{Y}/S)
,
L\cap\tau\neq\varnothing
implies
L\cap\tau\subset\operatorname{Relint}(\tau)
.

Given a test segment
L(p,q)
,
we assume for simplicity that
\sigma_{q}=\operatorname{Nef\textbullet}(\mathcal{Y}/S)
. By choosing a different model, which does not change the geometry of the Mori fan, we can always assume
that we are in this situation.

We now fix a connected component
\mathcal{C}
of
\mathcal{M}
with closure
\bar{\mathcal{C}}
.
Let
L=L(p,q)
be a line segment. We define

\operatorname{M}(L)=\{\sigma\in\operatorname{MF}_{\operatorname{max}}(\mathcal{Y}/S)\mid L\cap\sigma\neq\varnothing\},

\operatorname{I}(L)=\{\sigma\in\operatorname{M}(L)\mid L\cap\operatorname{Int}(\sigma)\neq\varnothing\}.

For a cone
\sigma
we write
F(\sigma)
for the set of subcones of the Mori fan of codimension
1
that are contained in
\sigma
, i.e. the facets of
\sigma
. We set

\operatorname{N}(L)=\{\sigma\in\operatorname{I}(L)\mid\forall\tau\in F(\sigma),L\cap\tau\subset\operatorname{Relint}(\tau)\}.

Note that the conditions to be in
I(L)
and
N(L)
are open conditions, so if
L^{\prime}
is a line segment contained in a small cylinder containing
L
, then
\operatorname{I}(L)\subset\operatorname{I}(L^{\prime})
and

\operatorname{N}(L)\subset\operatorname{N}(L^{\prime})
.
Also note that if
L
is a nice test segment,
\operatorname{M}(L)=\operatorname{I}(L)=\operatorname{N}(L)
.

Proposition 7.6
.

Let
\bar{\mathcal{C}}
be the closure of the connected component
\mathcal{C}
of
\mathcal{M}
.
Let
L=L(p,q)
be a nice
\mathcal{C}
-test segment where both
p
and
q
are divisors (with integral coefficients).
Let

\phi_{L}\colon\mathcal{Y}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{Y}_{1}\dashrightarrow\dots\dashrightarrow\mathcal{Y}_{n}

be the
sequence of flops induced by
L
.
Then
\gamma\in\operatorname{M}(L)
implies
\gamma\subset\bar{\mathcal{C}}
.

Proof.

Let
\sigma_{p}
and
\sigma_{q}
be the cones containing
p
and
q
, respectively. We assume
\sigma_{q}=\operatorname{Nef\textbullet}(\mathcal{Y}/S)
.
By Lemma
7.1
, there are two cases: either
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}_{\mathscr{P}}
or the two models corresponding to
\sigma_{p}
and
\sigma_{q}
are of type
\mathscr{T}
.

We first consider
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}_{\mathscr{P}}
. Then, as
L
is a nice test segment, it follows from the definition of
\mathcal{C}
that
\mathcal{Y}

and
\mathcal{Y}_{n}
have
dual intersection complex
\mathscr{P}
.
Then, by
Lemma
5.14
, all flops in the sequence
\phi_{L}

are of type I and thus all
\mathcal{Y}_{i}

have dual intersection complex
\mathscr{P}
. Hence all cones associated to
\mathcal{Y}_{i}
are in
\bar{\mathcal{C}}
, implying the claim.

Now consider the only other possible case, namely that the two models corresponding to
\sigma_{p}
and
\sigma_{q}
are of type
\mathscr{T}
.
Then
\mathcal{Y}_{\sigma_{p}}
and
\mathcal{Y}
have dual intersection complex
\mathscr{T}
.
By construction of
\mathcal{C}
we have a map
\psi\colon\mathcal{Y}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{Y}_{\sigma_{p}}
that is given by a series of type I flops. Note that these are not necessarily
F
-flops for a divisor
F\in\operatorname{Mov}(\mathcal{Y}/S)
.
However, the map
\psi
can also be written as a sequence

\mathcal{Y}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}_{1}\dashrightarrow\dots\dashrightarrow\mathcal{Y}_{\sigma_{p}}

of
F
-flops for some divisor
F\in\operatorname{Mov}(\mathcal{Y}/S)
.
Any flop in this sequence is necessarily of type I. By definition of the Mori fan, we have an isomorphism
\pi\colon\mathcal{Y}_{\sigma_{p}}\cong\mathcal{Y}_{n}
giving rise to a commutative diagram

\textstyle{\mathcal{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{L}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{Y}_{\sigma_{p}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{\mathcal{Y}_{n}.}

As
\psi
is a composition of type I flops, it maps smooth components of
\mathcal{Y}_{c}
to smooth components and the same is true for
\pi
. Hence
\phi_{L}

also maps the special component
(\mathcal{Y}_{c})_{\omega}
to the special component
((\mathcal{Y}_{n})_{c})_{\omega}
. We claim that this implies that all flops in the factorisation of
\phi

are of type I.
To show the claim, we assume there is a type II flop in the sequence and derive a contradiction. Let

(7.4)

\mathcal{Y}_{k}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{Y}_{k+1}\dashrightarrow\dots\dashrightarrow\mathcal{Y}_{n}

be the tail of the sequence where
\phi_{k}\colon\mathcal{Y}_{k}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{Y}_{k+1}
is the first type II flop.
Being type II, writing
(\mathcal{Y}_{k+1})_{c}=Y_{1}\cup Y_{2}\cup Y_{3}
, the flopped curve
C
is given by a component of the double curve, say by
D_{12}=Y_{1}\cap Y_{2}
on
Y_{1}
and
D_{21}
on
Y_{2}
, with
D_{21}^{2}=D_{12}^{2}=-1
. Also, the birational transform of
(\mathcal{Y}_{c})_{\omega}
on
\mathcal{Y}_{k+1}
is
Y_{3}
. Because
\mathcal{Y}_{n}
has dual intersection complex
\mathscr{T}
, there is at least
1
more type II flop in Sequence
7.4
, say
\phi_{l}
.
By Lemma
5.14
this is the only further type II flop. Because
\phi
maps
(\mathcal{Y}_{c})_{\omega}
to
((\mathcal{Y}_{n})_{c})_{\omega}
,
the exceptional locus
\operatorname{Ex}{\phi_{l}}
is given by the birational transform
C_{l}
of
C
on
\mathcal{Y}_{l}
. Also, the preimage of
C_{l}

under
\nu\colon(\mathcal{Y}_{l})_{c}^{\nu}\to(\mathcal{Y}_{l})_{c}
consist of two
(-1)
-curves. Hence all of the flops
\phi_{s}
,
l-1\geq s\geq k+1
have exceptional loci disjoint from the birational transform
C_{s}
of
C
on
\mathcal{Y}_{s}
. But this implies that for the birational transform
F_{l}
of
F
on
\mathcal{Y}_{l}
we have
F_{l}.C_{l}>0
, as
F_{k+1}.C>0
. But the contraction defining
\phi_{l}
contracts
C_{l}
, a contradiction. Hence
all
flops in
\phi
are of type I. In particular, by Equation
7.1
, all maximal cones met by
L
are in the closure of
\mathcal{C}
and thus
\gamma\subset\bar{\mathcal{C}}
.
∎

7.3.
Reduction

In this section we show that it is enough to check convexity on line segments defining sequences
of
F
-flops as in the previous section. The idea is as follows: if
L
is a line segment through two points in a connected component
\mathcal{C}
, by definition of the connected components, it is enough to check that any maximal cone
\sigma
of the Morifan met by
L
is contained in the closure
\bar{\mathcal{C}}
of
\mathcal{C}
. For this, one checks that there is a nice test segment
L^{\prime}
from some cone in
\mathcal{C}
that meets
\sigma
, which implies that all cones met by
L^{\prime}
are contained in
\bar{\mathcal{C}}
. Such a test segment can be found by wiggling the line segment
L
, as for one it is enough to check convexity on the interior of the connected components
and moreover
\operatorname{MF}(\mathcal{Y}/S)
has only finitely many cones. We give the details. The following lemma, which we include for completeness, is elementary.

Lemma 7.7
.

Let
U
be a convex set in a normed vector space
V
. Then the closure
\bar{U}
and the interior
U^{o}
are convex.

Lemma 7.8
.

Let
\bar{\mathcal{C}}
be the closure of a connected component of
\mathcal{M}
and let
p_{0},q_{0}
be points in the interior
\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{o}
. Set
L_{0}=L(p_{0},q_{0})
and assume

\gamma\in\operatorname{MF}_{\operatorname{max}}(\mathcal{Y}/S)
. If
L_{0}\cap\gamma\neq\varnothing
, then there is a
\mathcal{C}
-test segment
L
with
\gamma\in\operatorname{N}(L)
,
\operatorname{I}(L_{0})\subset\operatorname{I}(L)
and
\operatorname{N}(L_{0})\subset\operatorname{N}(L)
.

Proof.

Given data as in the statement of the lemma, suppose
L_{0}\cap\gamma\neq\varnothing
.
Then by translating both
p_{0}
and
q_{0}
by a small amount we can find points
p_{1},q_{1}\in\mathcal{C}^{o}

such that
L_{1}=(p_{1},q_{1})
intersects
\gamma
in its interior.
If the translation is small enough we can assume that
\operatorname{I}(L_{0})\subset\operatorname{I}(L_{1})
and
\operatorname{N}(L_{0})\subset\operatorname{N}(L_{1})
, as the relevant conditions are open and there are only finitely many cones in
\operatorname{MF}(\mathcal{Y}/S)
.

Let
\tau_{1}\in F(\gamma)
such that
L_{1}\cap\tau_{1}=\{x\}
, with
x\notin\operatorname{Relint}(\tau_{1})
. Let
\tau_{2}
be a facet of
\tau_{1}
with
x\in\tau_{2}
.
Let
v
be a vector tangent to
\tau_{1}

but not tangent to
\tau_{2}
to such that
x+v\in\operatorname{Relint}(\tau_{1})
.
Then by translating both
p_{1}
and
q_{2}
by some
\varepsilon v
we can find points
p_{2},q_{2}\in\mathcal{C}^{o}

such that
L_{2}=(p_{2},q_{2})
intersects
\gamma
in its interior,
L_{2}\cap\tau_{1}\subset\operatorname{Relint}(\tau_{1})
,
\operatorname{I}(L_{1})\subset\operatorname{I}(L_{2})

and
\operatorname{N}(L_{1})\subset\operatorname{N}(L_{2})
. Repeating the last step if necessary produces a line segment
L=(p^{\prime},q^{\prime})
with
\gamma\in\operatorname{N}(L)
,
\operatorname{I}(L_{0})\subset\operatorname{I}(L)

and
\operatorname{N}(L_{0})\subset\operatorname{N}(L)
.
We can also assume that neither
p^{\prime}
nor
q^{\prime}
is contained in a facet of
\operatorname{MF}(\mathcal{Y}/S)
. So there are cones
\sigma_{p_{1}},\sigma_{q_{1}}\in\operatorname{MF}_{\operatorname{max}}(\mathcal{Y}/S)
with
p_{1}\in\operatorname{Int}(\sigma_{p_{1}})
and
q_{2}\in\operatorname{Int}(\sigma_{q_{1}})
, so
L
is indeed a test segment.

∎

Lemma 7.9
.

Consider a connected component
\mathcal{C}
of
\mathcal{M}
and its closure
\bar{\mathcal{C}}
. Let
p_{0},q_{0}
be points in the interior
\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{o}
of the closure and set
L_{0}=L(p_{0},q_{0})
. Let
\gamma\in\operatorname{MF}_{\operatorname{max}}(\mathcal{Y}/S)
. If
L_{0}\cap\gamma\neq\varnothing
, then there exist
\mathbb{Z}
-divisors
p
and
q
such that
L(p,q)
is a nice test segment with
L\cap\gamma\neq\varnothing
.
Also,
\operatorname{N}(L_{0})\subset\operatorname{N}(L)
and
\operatorname{I}(L_{0})\subset\operatorname{I}(L)
.

Proof.

We first show the existence of a test sequence
L=L(p,q)
as claimed without the requirement that
p,q
are
\mathbb{Z}
-divisors.
Given data as in the statement of the lemma, by Lemma
7.8
, there is a test segment
L_{1}
with
\gamma\in\operatorname{N}(L_{1})
.
Arguing inductively,
let
L_{i}
be a test segment with
\gamma\in\operatorname{N}(L_{i})
.
Let
\sigma
be a cone in
\operatorname{M}(L_{i})\backslash\operatorname{N}(L_{i})
. Again by Lemma
7.8
, we find a test segment
L_{i+1}
with
\operatorname{I}(L_{i})\subset\operatorname{I}(L_{i+1})
and
\operatorname{N}(L_{i})\subset\operatorname{N}(L_{i+1})
and
|\operatorname{N}(L_{i})|<|\operatorname{N}(L_{i+1})|
.
Hence we obtain a sequence
\{L_{i}\}_{i}
of test segments with
|\operatorname{N}(L_{i})|<|\operatorname{N}(L_{i+1})|
. As
|\operatorname{MF}_{\operatorname{max}}(\mathcal{Y}/S)|<\infty
, this is a finite sequence
\{L_{1},L_{2},\dots,L_{n}\}
, and thus
\operatorname{M}(L_{n})=\operatorname{N}(L_{n})
. Setting
L=L_{n}
proves the claim.

Now, given
L=L(p,q)
, perturbing a little, we can assume that
p,q
are classes of
\mathbb{Q}
-divisors. Then there is an
n
such that
L(np,nq)

is a test segment with the desired properties.
∎

Theorem 7.10
.

Let
\mathcal{Y}\to S
be a model of the DNV family of degree
2
. The closures
\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{i},i=1,\dots 4
of the connected components of
\mathcal{M}
are convex cones. The collection

\Sigma_{\mathcal{M}}=\{\sigma\subset\mathcal{M}\mid\sigma\text{ is a face of some }\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{i}\}

defines a finite fan of rational polyhedral cones.

Definition 7.11
.

The fan
\Sigma_{\mathcal{M}}
is the
secondary fan
of
\mathcal{Y}
, denoted by
\operatorname{MF}^{\operatorname{2nd}}(\mathcal{Y}/S)
.

Proof of Theorem
7.10
.

All that remains to be shown is that the connected components of
\mathcal{M}
have convex closures and that
\bar{C}_{i}\cap\bar{C}_{j}\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{M}}
.
Let
\mathcal{C}
be a connected component. It is enough to show that its interior
\mathcal{C}^{o}
is convex.
Let
p_{0},q_{0}
be points in
\mathcal{C}^{o}
. Let
\gamma
be a maximal cone meeting
L=L(p_{0},q_{0})
. By Lemma
7.9
, we find a nice

\bar{\mathcal{C}}
-test segment
L(p,q)
with
\gamma\in\operatorname{N}(L(p,q))
, where
p
and
q
can be chosen to be classes of divisors.
By Proposition
7.6
,
\gamma\subset\bar{\mathcal{C}}
. Let
\partial\operatorname{Mov}(\mathcal{Y}/S)
be the boundary of the moving cone.
Now, we can decompose
\bar{\mathcal{C}}

as

\bar{\mathcal{C}}=\mathcal{C}^{0}\cup B_{1}\cup B_{2}

where
B_{1}\subset\partial\operatorname{Mov}(\mathcal{Y}/S)

and
B_{2}\subset\cup_{\tau\in\mathcal{F}}|\tau|
.
where
\mathcal{F}
is the set of all facets of type II of the Mori fan.
By convexity of
\operatorname{Mov}(\mathcal{Y}/S)

it follows that
L\subset\operatorname{Mov}(\mathcal{Y}/S)^{o}
. As we have just seen, all maximal cones
\gamma
meeting
L=L(p_{0},q_{0})
are contained in
\bar{\mathcal{C}}
. It follows that all facets
\tau
met by
L
are of type I, as else the maximal cones containing
\tau
would correspond to models of the DNV family that do not have the same class. So
L\cap B_{2}=\varnothing

and thus
L\subset\mathcal{C}^{o}
, implying that
\mathcal{C}^{o}
is convex. It follows from Lemma
7.7
that
\bar{\mathcal{C}}
is convex.
By Proposition
7.4
, we obtain
4
maximal cones
\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{i},i=1,\dots 4
. It remains to check that
\Sigma_{\mathcal{M}}
is indeed a fan. This will follow from the following description of the
\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{i}
. Assume
\mathcal{C}_{1}=\mathcal{C}_{\mathscr{P}}
.
From the construction, we immediately have the following description of the maximal cones.
For each maximal cone
\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{i}

the facets contained in
\partial\operatorname{Mov}(\mathcal{Y}/S)
are unions of maximal cones in

\cup_{\{\sigma\in\operatorname{MF}_{\operatorname{max}}(\mathcal{Y}/S)\mid\sigma\subset\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{i}\}}\partial\operatorname{Mov}(\mathcal{Y}/S)\cap\sigma.

It follows from the convexity of the cones
\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{i}
, that each
\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{i}
,
i=2,3,4
has a unique facet
H_{\mathcal{C}_{i}}
meeting the interior of
\operatorname{Mov}(\mathcal{Y}/S)
.
H_{\mathcal{C}_{i}}
is the union of type II facets that are contained in the
\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{i}
, formally

H_{\mathcal{C}_{i}}=\cup_{\tau\in\mathcal{F}:\tau\subset\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{i}}|\tau|.

For
i=1
, one has again the boundary facets and also the facets
H_{\mathcal{C}_{i}}
.
Note that by definition of
\mathcal{M}
the intersection of two cones of type
\mathscr{T}
of
\Sigma_{\mathcal{M}}
is at least of codimension
2
.
It follows
\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{i}\cap\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{j}\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{M}}
.
So
\Sigma_{\mathcal{M}}
is indeed a fan. Being a coarsening of a finite fan consisting of rational polyhedral cones, all cones of
\Sigma_{\mathcal{M}}
are also rational polyhedral.
∎

Figure 21.
A schematic picture of
\operatorname{MF}^{\operatorname{2nd}}(\mathcal{Y}/S)
. The central cone is
\mathcal{C}_{\mathscr{P}}
.

Remark 7.12
.

The
S_{3}
-action on
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathscr{P}}
induces an action on the maximal cones of
\operatorname{MF}^{\operatorname{2nd}}(\mathcal{Y}/S)
. Write
\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{i},i=1,\dots 4
for the maximal cones. Assume
\mathcal{C}_{1}=\mathcal{C}_{\mathscr{P}}
. As
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathscr{P}}
has as unique associated cone, this action leaves
\mathcal{C}_{\mathscr{P}}
invariant. Combining the arguments in Proposition
7.6
and Proposition
5.43
shows that the
S_{3}
-action is indeed a permutation action on the cones
\bar{C}_{i},i=2,\dots 4
.
Figure
21
provides a symbolic description of the structure of
\operatorname{MF}^{\operatorname{2nd}}(\mathcal{Y}/S)
.
